The debugger allows you to control the execution flow of the test and the tested code, inspect variable values, and track the call stack to understand exactly why the test is failing or behaving unexpectedly.\n\n---\n\n## ≡ƒ¢á∩╕Å Key Concepts of Debugging Tests\n\nThe process involves several essential actions supported by most Integrated Development Environments (IDEs) and testing frameworks:\n\n* **Setting Breakpoints:** A **breakpoint** is a marker you place in your code or test script where you want execution to intentionally pause. When the debugger hits a breakpoint, the test execution stops, allowing you to examine the program's state [^1][^2].\n* **Stepping Through Code:** Once execution is paused, you can control how the code runs line-by-line using \"step\" commands [^1]:\n    * **Step Over:** Executes the current line of code and moves to the next line. If the current line is a function call, it runs the entire function without stopping inside it.\n    * **Step Into:** Executes the current line. If it's a function call, the debugger steps *inside* the function, allowing you to trace its execution.\n    * **Step Out:** Continues execution until the current function returns, then pauses on the next line in the calling function.\n    * **Continue/Run:** Resumes execution until the next breakpoint is hit or the program/test ends.\n* **Inspecting Variables:** While paused at a breakpoint, you can **inspect** the current values of variables and expressions. This is crucial for verifying if the data is what you expect it to be at that point in the code [^2][^3].\n* **Call Stack:** The **call stack** shows the sequence of function calls that led to the current point of execution. This helps you understand the execution path and the context in which the error occurred [^2].\n\n---\n\n## ≡ƒÆ╗ Debugging Tools and Environments\n\nThe specific steps for debugging depend on the programming language and the tools you are using. Common environments and frameworks support debugging:\n\n| Environment/Framework | Common Debugging Methods |\n| :--- | :--- |\n| **Visual Studio** (C#, C++, etc.) | Use **Test Explorer** to right-click and select \"Debug\" on a test method. Seamlessly steps between test code and project code [^1]. |\n| **JetBrains IDEs** (IntelliJ IDEA, WebStorm, etc.) | Set a breakpoint, then run the test using the **Debug** option. Use the \"Evaluate\" feature to check expressions [^3][^8]. |\n| **VS Code** (JavaScript, Python, etc.) | Set breakpoints and use a debug configuration (e.g., for Jest or Vitest) to start the debugger with F5 [^4][^8][^9]. |\n| **TestComplete** | Uses a dedicated **keyword test debugger** and **script debugger** with panels for Locals, Watch List, and Call Stack [^2]. |\n| **Playwright** (Web Testing) | Set breakpoints or use `page.pause()` to stop execution and then run the test in debug mode (often with a `--debug` flag or a specific debugger profile) [^5]. |\n\nThis practice is the most effective way to pinpoint the **root cause** of a failing test, as opposed to simply observing the failure message.\n\nIs there a specific programming language or testing framework you're using for
